# Root-cause log — every defect gets 5 whys, and finding a cause RESTARTS the count

**Law (operator 2026-07-19):** *"The 5 levels of why is universal, it's with everything we do.
If we get to the 2nd why and we find the defect, then the 5-level why starts again, until we
can verify everything is fixed."*

Surface fixes leave landmines. A cause found at why-2 is not the root — it is a new defect,
and a new defect gets its own five whys. The chain terminates only when a why produces no
new defect **and** the fix is verified.

**FRONT-LOADED and UNIVERSAL (operator 2026-07-26, non-negotiable).** The law fires the
INSTANT an issue is found — while coding, reviewing, auditing, testing, at ANY time — not at
commit and not afterward. The RC row is opened at DISCOVERY, *before* the fix, and the fix
repairs the ROOT end-to-end. The scope is the whole repo, every file, every subsystem — it is
NOT narrowable to a subsystem. Mechanically backstopped by
`check_recursive_five_why_front_loaded` (ENFORCED): any commit staging a real change to a
tracked `.py` file must co-stage a real `| RC-` row here (a cosmetic touch does not satisfy
it; the row's quality is enforced by `five_why_recursive_lock`). Three layers — discovery-time
trigger, pre-commit backstop, row-quality lock — so a fix cannot reach a commit without its
recursive root cause. Water cannot enter.


**Boundary vs `governance/unproven_register.md`.** This file holds DEFECTS IN OUR CODE
("why did this break?"). The register holds CLAIMS ABOUT THE WORLD ("is this true of the
market, the data, or the vendor?"). A defect never belongs in the register and a claim
never belongs here. Enforced by `check_no_governance_duplication`.

## Rules

1. Every defect opens an entry here with a `RC-<n>` id.
2. Ask why. If the answer names a **new defect**, record it, fix it, and **restart at why-1
   for that new defect** in its own entry (linked as a child).
3. An entry may close only when: five consecutive whys produce no new defect, AND the fix
   has same-session verification evidence, AND every child entry is closed.
4. `status` is `OPEN` until then. `tools/check_institutional_correctness.py::check_root_cause_log`
   (ENFORCED) blocks commits on any `OPEN` entry past its `due` date. **RC-228 (document-without-resolve):**
   a newly ADDED OPEN row must name a resolve path (`FIXED:` / `NEXT-DEPTH:` / `OUT-OF-SCOPE:`) in
   the fix cell; staging `pm_mission.json` to DONE/COMPLETE/idle while any OPEN RC names that
   `mission_id` BLOCKs (`check_rc_document_without_resolve`). Do not mass-fake CLOSE — honest
   PARTIAL + tracker is the legal incomplete form.
5. "I didn't check", "the sign was inverted", "I didn't do that right" are **not** root causes.
   They are symptoms of a missing mechanical check — keep asking why until you reach the
   check that was absent, then add it.
6. **No terminal nulls (operator law 2026-07-24, machine-forced by `no_terminal_null`):**
   there is no terminal state of "no solutions exist" — there is only engineering depth
   yet to be unlocked. A wall may be stated only by naming the door: surrender vocabulary
   in a row requires `NEXT-DEPTH: <the unlock>`, and a null-verdict study report requires
   a `next_depth` field naming the successor bet, data unlock, or generator.

**Row format (machine-parsed — keep the columns):**
`| id | status | opened | due | defect | why-chain -> root | fix + verification |`

| id | status | opened | due | defect | why-chain -> root | fix + verification |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| RC-282 | OPEN | 2026-08-07 | 2026-08-08 | ONE UNDATED CACHE ENTRY PRODUCES TWO OPPOSITE FRESHNESS VERDICTS, AND THE ONE THE OPERATOR SEES SAYS FRESH. `_analytics_generated_ts` (server.py:1687) returns `float(entry.get("generated_at") or entry.get("ts") or 0.0)`, and its three callers each invent a different meaning for that 0.0. MEASURED by Cursor's adversarial audit, executing the functions from their AST: an entry carrying `ms_dict` but neither timestamp yields `{'analytics_generated_at': None, 'analytics_age_sec': 0.0, 'analytics_stale': False, 'analytics_refresh_due': False}` — server.py:1784 computes `age = max(0.0, now - gen_ts) if gen_ts > 0 else 0.0`, so an undatable bundle is published as brand new AND not due for refresh. The SAME helper at server.py:10364 computes `max(0.0, now - _analytics_generated_ts(entry))`, gets ~1.8e9 seconds, and marks the entry stale. Two call sites, one sentinel, opposite verdicts about the identical cache entry — and the fresh one is the Tier-C freshness contract the operator's card reads, while the honest one is only the internal stale-serve marker. The `else` branch at 1801-1806 already does the right thing for a MISSING bundle (`age_sec: None`, `stale: True`); a bundle that exists but cannot be dated falls into the fresh path instead. | (1) an undated analytics bundle renders as fresh -> (2) because the freshness branch converts a 0.0 sentinel into age zero rather than treating it as unknown -> (3) because the helper returns a float unconditionally, so "no timestamp" and "computed at the epoch" are the same value by the time any caller sees it -> (4) because a numeric sentinel makes absence LOOK handled at every call site — each caller wrote arithmetic that is locally reasonable, and the two happen to fall opposite ways, which is why the disagreement survived: nothing compares them and each looks correct in isolation -> (5) ROOT: this is RC-274's root reaching a THIRD surface. A field has one producer, but ABSENCE is still re-decided at every read, and here the re-decision is invisible because both readings are plausible arithmetic on the same number. RC-274 fixed the storage and render paths and RC-281 fixed three hand-written exemptions; none of them removed the SENTINEL, which is the mechanism that lets absence be re-interpreted at all. | FIXED: reproduced and re-verified by `.venv/Scripts/python.exe -m pytest tests/test_analytics_undated_is_not_fresh_v1.py -q` (8 passed), which drives `_attach_analytics_freshness_contract` with an entry carrying `ms_dict` and no timestamp — before: `age_sec 0.0, stale False, refresh_due False`; after: `age_sec None, stale True, refresh_due True`; dated controls unchanged (5s old -> stale False, 99999s old -> stale True). `_analytics_generated_ts` now returns an optional float via `float_positive_or_none`, so absence cannot be mistaken for a timestamp, and all three callers state what absence means — the freshness contract publishes `analytics_age_sec: None`, `analytics_stale: True`, `analytics_refresh_due: True` for an undatable bundle, matching what the missing-bundle branch beside it already does; the completed-fetch path at 2553 guards on `is not None` instead of `> 0`; the stale-serve marker at 10364 keeps its existing fail-closed verdict but reaches it by stating the rule rather than by arithmetic on 1970. Verified by re-running Cursor's probe to the opposite result and by co-staged tests asserting an undated entry is stale on BOTH surfaces, plus a negative control that a dated entry is still fresh. |

| RC-279 | CLOSED | 2026-08-06 | 2026-08-07 | RECURRENCE: stale-test-double-vs-callee-signature — second occurrence, RC-239 being the first, in THE SAME FILE. What is structurally different: RC-239 repaired three doubles by hand and left ten identical ones behind, so this row does not repeat that fix; it removes the coupling that makes hand-repair necessary. TEN OF THE FOURTEEN TESTS IN `tests/test_chain_gate_v2.py` FAIL WITH ONE ERROR AND IT IS NOT A PRODUCT DEFECT: `TypeError: <lambda>() got an unexpected keyword argument 'to_date'`. MEASURED by `.venv/Scripts/python.exe -m pytest tests/test_chain_gate_v2.py -q` -> 10 failed, 4 passed. The real callee is `safe_get_chain(client, ticker, *, strike_count=20, from_date=None, to_date=None)` and the gated call site at server.py:898 passes `to_date=`, while all 13 monkeypatched doubles in this file are pinned to `(client, ticker, *, strike_count)`. The concurrency behaviour these tests exist to protect -- coalescing, cross-ticker isolation, breaker trip and recovery, deadlock under mixed load -- has been UNTESTED since the signature changed, and worse than untested: the suite was red for a reason that reads like a product failure, so a real regression in the gate would have been indistinguishable from the noise. | (1) ten tests fail on a keyword the product legitimately added -> (2) because each double independently restates the callee's parameter list -> (3) because a hand-written `lambda c, t, *, strike_count: "OK"` is the shortest thing that works the day it is written, and there are 13 of them, so the cost of a signature change is 13 edits nobody is reminded to make -> (4) because RC-239 hit exactly this and fixed the three doubles that happened to be failing at that moment, which restored green and left the other ten carrying the identical defect -- a repair scoped to the symptom that presented rather than to the shape shared by every double in the file -> (5) ROOT: nothing makes a test double PROVE it can still stand in for what it replaces. `monkeypatch.setattr` accepts any callable, so a double is validated only by being called, and a double that is wrong fails inside the code under test where the error looks like the subject's fault. The repo has no rule that a substitute must be signature-compatible with its original, and without one every callee signature change silently arms every double that names its parameters. | FIXED: rewrite all 13 doubles to `(client, ticker, **kwargs)` so no future keyword can break them, and add `test_every_double_can_stand_in_for_the_real_safe_get_chain`, which binds each double against `inspect.signature(server.safe_get_chain)` with the arguments the gated call site actually passes — so a double that drifts fails ITS OWN test with a clear message instead of failing ten behavioural tests with a TypeError raised inside a worker thread. Verified by `.venv/Scripts/python.exe -m pytest tests/test_chain_gate_v2.py -q` reaching 16 passed (14 originals + 2 new locks), and by a mutation check: restoring `_boom` to `*, strike_count` failed the compatibility test naming its own line, confirmed and reverted. VIOLATION: no lock required a test double to remain call-compatible with the callee it replaces — `monkeypatch.setattr` accepts any callable, so a substitute was validated only by being invoked, and when it was wrong the TypeError surfaced inside the subject (and inside a worker thread) where it read as a product failure. RC-239 was the same class and its repair was scoped to the three doubles then failing, which is why the control did not exist afterwards either. TIGHTENED: `test_every_double_can_stand_in_for_the_real_safe_get_chain` now reads the forwarded keyword set out of `_gated_safe_get_chain`'s AST rather than from a hand-kept list, enumerates every double installed in the file, and fails naming the offending line; `test_the_gated_call_site_still_matches_the_real_callee` binds those same keywords against the real signature so producer and call site cannot drift apart silently. END-TO-END: callee signature (`safe_get_chain`) -> gated call site (`_gated_safe_get_chain`) -> the 13 substitutes installed over it -> the 14 behavioural assertions that consume them; the whole strip is now checked in one place instead of being discovered one TypeError at a time. FIXED: tests/test_chain_gate_v2.py, carried by cited commit `bd9a9604`; `server.py` is named here only as the call site read for context and was changed by cited commit `23aa2a5d`, not by this closure. |

Sibling defect in the same workstream: `tools/relabel_non_trading_sessions_v1.py` was committed with zero referrers and no schedule -- it is the cleanup tool for the weekend rows these two functions create, so the repair was written before the leak was stopped. | (1) weekend rows reach `option_chain_morning_full` and `option_chain_accrual` -> (2) because both writers ask "is the clock inside the capture window" and never "is the market open today" -> (3) because a minutes-since-midnight test READS like a session test: 09:30-11:30 ET is the morning capture span, and on five days in seven the two questions give the same answer -> (4) because the calendar authority was applied at the READ boundary instead -- `get_forces` filters candidates through `is_trading_day_et`, which makes the console LOOK correct while the table underneath fills with rows no reader admits to, so the defect is invisible from every surface -> (5) ROOT: the same shape as RC-274 one row down. There, ABSENCE was re-decided at every read; here, WHAT COUNTS AS A SESSION is re-decided at every boundary, and the repo has a single calendar authority that write paths are not required to consult. RC-176 already found this exact pair (clock AND calendar) in desk_store's readers and fixed it there; nothing carried the finding to the writers, because a root cause closes against the sites it was found at rather than against the class. | FIXED: call `is_trading_day_et(et_date)` in both writers immediately after `et_date_and_mins`, before any window test, returning `{"status": "skipped", "reason": "non_trading_day"}` -- the calendar decides whether a session exists at all, so it is a precondition to the clock question, not a peer of it. Verified by `.venv/Scripts/python.exe -m pytest tests/test_rc193_morning_full_calendar_gate_v1.py tests/test_rc191_zero_debt_product_v1.py -q` and by a mutation check: removing either guard must re-fail its test. NEXT-DEPTH: remember the last-good basis per ticker and start the ladder there, re-probing `full` at most once per ET day, which removes the recurring probe entirely. MEASURED n=1 PROVEN. |

The live endpoint showed the same signature: `/api/liquidity-snapshot` returned PD_POC == PD_VAL for BOTH SPY (729.57) and QQQ (661.66) — a degenerate value area, which is what a sparse one-bin-per-bar histogram produces. | (1) why is POC 22 points from where price is trading? -> (2) each bar contributes to exactly ONE price bin, so the profile has at most one bin per bar and its peak is wherever typical prices happened to coincide, not where volume rested -> (3) why one bin? the construction models a bar as a point event at (H+L+C)/3, but a bar is an INTERVAL: its volume traded across [low, high] and the profile is precisely the record of where -> (4) NEW DEFECT found while tracing callers: `market_context._volume_profile_poc_vah_val` is a SECOND, independent copy of the same dump, feeding `pd_poc/pd_vah/pd_val` and `today_poc/today_vah/today_val` — two copies of a wrong construction are two wrong answers that can also disagree with each other -> (5) ROOT: the bar was modelled as a point rather than an interval, and that modelling error was duplicated instead of owned, so there was no single place where it could be corrected. | ONE construction in `liquidity_models.volume_profile_poc_vah_val` — the module both callers can import with no cycle (pure dataclasses; it already owns `PlaybookConfig.tick_size`/`value_area_percent`). Each bar's volume is distributed uniformly across the bins spanning [low, high]; a flat bar (high == low) keeps all volume at its one traded price. Bins are addressed by INTEGER index `round(price/tick)` because accumulating float bin prices as dict keys lets 724.9999999 and 725.0 become two bins for one price, fragmenting the profile and moving the POC. Uniform is the honest within-bar model: we have no trade prints (the Schwab streamer carries none), so any other shape would be invented rather than measured — uniform asserts only what the bar states, that this volume traded somewhere in [low, high]. `MAX_BINS_PER_BAR = 5000` bounds a pathological wide bar by spreading across evenly-spaced bins covering the same range — a work cap, never a licence to dump. Both `liquidity_value_engine._volume_profile_poc_vah_val` (ndigits=4) and `market_context._volume_profile_poc_vah_val` (ndigits=2, preserving its original rounding) are now thin delegates, so no caller changed. END-TO-END: `liquidity_models.volume_profile_poc_vah_val` (the ONE construction) -> `liquidity_value_engine._volume_profile_poc_vah_val` -> `get_previous_day_levels` (PD_POC/PD_VAH/PD_VAL) and `compute_volume_profile_levels` -> `build_opening_snapshot` / `build_midday_snapshot` / `build_live_snapshot` -> `generate_liquidity_value_snapshot` -> `/api/liquidity-snapshot` `raw_levels_used`; and in parallel -> `market_context._volume_profile_poc_vah_val` -> `pd_poc/pd_vah/pd_val` + `today_poc/today_vah/today_val` on the price-levels payload. Both former copies now delegate; no third implementation exists (caller sweep over every tracked .py this turn found exactly the two). FIXED: `liquidity_value_engine._volume_profile_poc_vah_val`, `market_context._volume_profile_poc_vah_val`, and the six call sites they feed (`liquidity_value_engine.py` lines 308/884/1004/1194 via the two entry points, `market_context.py` lines 1119/1162). MEASURED — `.venv/Scripts/python.exe -m pytest tests/test_chain_accrual_and_storm1_v1.py -q` -> 14 passed: both named boundaries inclusive, 09:14 and 16:16 rejected, all premarket minutes 09:15-09:29 accepted, ET/CT equivalence proven by conversion, a 4-observation series spanning minutes 555..975 in one day (which the old table could not hold), fail-closed on six classes of unusable row, and storm1 verified against a hand-worked 3-strike case where neither the biggest volume nor the biggest gamma wins (winner storm1=4 at k=200) plus a far-strike case where the winner sits 40 percent from spot. `python -m pytest tests/test_gamma_fullchain_strikes_v1.py -q` -> 6 passed, so the widened span did not strand the capture-span validators. ROOT FOUND 2026-08-04 (RC-243, post-RTH under the PM GO): the tier-1 lock was being held for the whole GOVERNED-OUTCOME RECOMPUTE, not merely the bar write - `_refresh_governed_outcomes_after_bar_mutation` ran inside `_do()` under `_TIER1_SNAPSHOT_WRITE_LOCK` - which is what turns a fast insert into a 38-180s hold on a 27GB file and why extra bar workers made the console SLOWER (they queue behind each other's recomputes). This row's own test asserted that contract on 2026-07-31 and had been RED ever since: specified, tested, never landed. FIXED in db.py (post-unlock refresh on a separate connection) and server.py (BARS_WORKERS 3 -> 2), with 53 tests green across the bar/outcome path. Row stays PARTIAL until a LIVE RTH session is measured with the fix in place - the post-close window cannot prove it, since the recent-window contention counter already read 0 with the driver dormant. FIXED: server.py `_CandleAccumulator.tick` and its `_prev_total_vol_ts` state (the delta attribution site), the ACCUM_VOL_MAX_ATTRIBUTION_GAP_SEC bound, and the downstream victims that consumed the blown minutes - db.upsert_1m_bars -> price_bars_1m -> desk_store.materialize_dollar_volume ADV. ATTRIBUTION MEASURED, which is what made the root certain: over the same ticker and period the accumulator produced 601 non-auction 10x-neighbourhood volume spikes in 24,284 bars (2.5%) against the vendor's own 1m bars at 8 in 13,017 (0.06%) - a 40x rate from the same market, isolating the attribution rather than the feed; separately the single largest MSFT bar (58.3M) was EXONERATED as the real 16:01 closing auction, with the classic into-the-close ramp beside it, so the row's blanket 'impossible values' framing was itself partly wrong. END-TO-END: Schwab quote totalVolume -> tick() gap-bounded delta -> bar volume -> upsert_1m_bars -> price_bars_1m -> Desk ADV median. The detector gap is the same root at the syntax layer - the rule matches one SPELLING of the action instead of resolving what the command will actually do, so proof-binding and action-detection fail for the identical reason. | Reproduce all four before and after any change with `python -c "import sys,json,subprocess,os; sys.path.insert(0,'tools'); import operator_law_guard as G; print(G.bash_violations('git -C . commit -m x', []))"` and by driving tools/operator_law_guard.py through its stdin payload with an isolated session_id. AFFECTED: repository EdWebConsole (false negatives one and three weaken its own commit protection) and repository IEOS (false positive blocks its mission class entirely); mission classes affected are every IEOS-targeted commit and every Ed Console commit whose turn touched another checkout. GUARANTEES BROKEN: RC-93 commit-before-proof, and by the same ledger the RC-125 live-probe clause and the self-audit and research Stop clauses inherit the same session-bearer weakness. NEXT-DEPTH: due 2026-08-07 - resolve the target repository from the actual execution context (explicit path flags, leading directory changes, and the tool payload working directory when it supplies one), normalise it for Windows case and slash style, bind every ledger entry to that identity, require repository-MATCHING proof for repository-scoped rules, decide applicability PER RULE so universally-applicable destructive-git and lock-disable protections keep firing everywhere, fail OPENLY and loudly when identity cannot be resolved rather than silently assuming this repository, express the RC-93 scope through a durable applicability declaration rather than a hardcoded name, and repair the commit detector so a flagged form cannot walk past it. ADVERSARIAL PROOF REQUIRED before this row closes: negative controls that FAIL against the pre-fix implementation, plus resolution adversaries covering quoted paths, spaces, relative paths, mixed slash styles, Windows case variants, chained commands, nonexistent paths, paths outside any repository, and malformed quoting. FIFTH FAILURE, OBSERVED BY THE GUARD FIRING ON THIS VERY ROW'S AUTHOR 2026-08-05: an Edit that a LATER hook REFUSED was still written into the ledger as a production edit, because `main` calls `_record` as soon as `edit_violations` returns clean and hook chains run in sequence - so the attempted edit to tools/operator_law_guard.py, which tools/process_lock_guard.py then BLOCKED and which therefore never touched the disk, made the Stop clause demand a self adversarial audit for a change that does not exist. The ledger records INTENT rather than OUTCOME, so a refused action still accrues obligations; the same shape would let a blocked edit satisfy or trigger any ledger-driven clause. SIXTH FAILURE, FOUND IN MY OWN FIX BY READING THE LIVE LEDGER RATHER THAN TRUSTING THE TESTS, 2026-08-05: the first cut of the resolver treated a leading slash as a rooted Windows path, but the Bash tool on this host IS Git Bash, so the ORDINARY spelling is cd slash c slash Users slash - and Windows resolves that to a C colon backslash c backslash Users path which does not exist. Measured on the live ledger: bash entries with a resolved repository were 0 of 14, so every commit would have failed OPENLY as unresolved and the fix would have been inert-by-strictness rather than working. Repaired with an MSYS and cygdrive drive translation, after which the live hook resolved the very next command; three locking tests cover the msys, cygdrive, and equivalence cases. SETTLED THE SAME WAY, a question the previous turn had to leave open: the Claude Code PreToolUse payload DOES carry a working directory - DEMONSTRATED DETERMINISTICALLY, no statistical verdict is claimed or possible here, by running a plain command carrying no directory change and no path flag and observing the live ledger record repo c colon slash users slash evarg slash documents slash trading slash edwebconsole, which no other source could have supplied. OUT-OF-SCOPE: nothing - this row owns the whole surface. VISIBLE_SURFACE: none. CLOSED_WITH_EVIDENCE 2026-08-05 under a Product Owner scope determination that authorised tools and tests and governance for this repair only. FIXED: tools/operator_law_guard.py carries the whole repair - resolve_target_repo plus normalize_repo plus repo_root_of resolve identity from the command's own execution context with precedence explicit path flag then directory change then the tool payload working directory and never a fallback to the guard's own checkout; is_git_commit replaces the adjacency pattern with a token test; _record and _record_edit_attempt bind every ledger entry to a normalised repository; _has_verification requires a matching repository while _has_verification_any keeps the Stop clauses session-scoped and behaviourally unchanged; _edit_took_effect compares an mtime captured BEFORE the tool ran so a refused edit accrues no obligation while a legacy row with no baseline keeps its old meaning; _msys_to_windows translates the Git Bash and cygdrive drive spellings this host actually uses; rc93_applies_to reads scope from data. governance/guard_applicability.json declares the ten fields of the governing applicability model - mechanism identity, authority, applicable repositories, mission classes, components, triggers, exclusions, precedence, conflict behaviour, review conditions - plus the Product Owner determination, and identifies repositories by CONTENT MARKERS so a clone at any path is the same repository and no absolute path is hardcoded anywhere. tests/test_operator_law_guard_repo_scope_v1.py is the dedicated suite. tests/test_enforced_check_negative_controls_v1.py has its existing battery updated to supply the working directory and a repository-bound ledger, with every banned and sanctioned SPELLING unchanged. END-TO-END: Claude Code PreToolUse payload carrying session_id and tool_name and tool_input and cwd -> resolve_target_repo -> normalised repository identity -> universal rules evaluated unconditionally -> repository-scoped RC-93 evaluated only where the declaration says it governs -> ledger entry bound to that identity -> a later commit authorised only by proof carrying the SAME identity -> Stop clauses reading outcome-confirmed edits. VIOLATION: RC-93's own mechanism. The guard was registered globally and read as universally applicable, which the governing model forbids - global wiring is not a declaration of scope - and its REPO constant was computed and never consulted by a single rule, so authorisation was bound to the SESSION rather than to the SUBJECT of the action. No lock detected this because every existing control tested the guard's SPELLINGS, never its scope, and no check asked whether a mechanism that fires everywhere had declared that it governs everywhere. TIGHTENED: scope is now declared as data and asserted by tests that a hardcoded repository exception cannot satisfy - the no-hardcoded-exception control is matched by AST rather than by string search, so the guard may still DOCUMENT the measured failures without the control firing on its own record. MEASURED and VERIFIED this turn: `python -m pytest tests/test_operator_law_guard_repo_scope_v1.py tests/test_enforced_check_negative_controls_v1.py tests/test_ui_mockup_lock_v1.py tests/test_operating_process_lock_v1.py tests/test_writer_drift_lock_v1.py tests/test_stop_guard_v1.py tests/test_five_why_recursive_lock_v1.py tests/test_reset_guard_v1.py tests/test_honesty_guard_v1.py tests/test_log_law_v1.py -q` -> 252 passed, of which 82 are this row's dedicated suite; `python tools/check_institutional_correctness.py` -> GATE PASS with enforced checks clean; `python -m ruff check tools/operator_law_guard.py tests/test_operator_law_guard_repo_scope_v1.py` -> All checks passed. ALL SIX REPRODUCED FAILURES NOW BEHAVE CORRECTLY, driven through the real hook entrypoint with isolated session ledgers: an Ed Console commit with no proof BLOCKS; the same commit written as git dash C dot or with dash dash git-dir now BLOCKS where both returned exit 0 before; proof recorded from another checkout no longer authorises an Ed Console commit; matching Ed Console proof permits it; an IEOS-targeted commit is no longer judged by RC-93 where it previously exited 2; an unresolvable target FAILS OPENLY with the reason named rather than being silently treated as this repository; and an Edit is recorded as an attempt with its pre-edit mtime so a refused edit raises no self-audit obligation. RETAINED UNIVERSAL PROTECTIONS, asserted rather than assumed: destructive git, lock-disable, blind staging, heredoc and payload and PowerShell unsafe writes, and the no-grep law all still fire inside an unrelated repository AND when repository identity is unresolved, because applicability is decided per rule and the guard never returns early; the operator-only escape remains operator-only; non-commit command behaviour is unchanged. LIMITATIONS: the six failures and the fix are proven on this host and its two shells - other shells' directory syntaxes are untested; cross-repository behaviour is proven against a marker-free temporary repository and against IEOS-targeted command STRINGS, and no commit was ever executed inside IEOS by this row; the Stop clauses remain deliberately session-scoped, so a turn that verifies repository A and edits repository B still satisfies them, which is correct for a per-turn question but is not repository binding. CODE CITED, so the reader can tell the two apart: the files this commit CARRIES are tools/operator_law_guard.py and tests/test_operator_law_guard_repo_scope_v1.py and tests/test_enforced_check_negative_controls_v1.py and governance/guard_applicability.json. FOUND 2026-08-05 while executing the operator's durable-closure mission, whose written instruction was to record the status cell as CLOSED_WITH_EVIDENCE - a token that reads STRONGER than CLOSED to a human and is INVISIBLE to every checker. | RC-254 | CLOSED | 2026-08-04 | 2026-08-05 | THE PRE-COMMIT GATE HAS BEEN REPORTING PASS WITHOUT RUNNING: tools/run_with_repo_venv.py re-execs its target under the repo venv with os.execv. On POSIX that replaces the process and the target's exit status becomes the caller's. On WINDOWS there is no exec - CPython maps os.execv onto the CRT spawn family, so the parent returns to its caller IMMEDIATELY with status 0 while the target runs DETACHED and its exit code is discarded. Every pre-commit hook routed through that wrapper has therefore been reporting 'Passed' unconditionally: venv-parity, market-correctness, operating-process, and - worst - institutional-correctness, which reaches it through tools/precommit_institutional.py, itself a second os.execv. The repo's ONE gate, the lock every other rule defers to, has not been able to block a commit on this host. MEASURED 2026-08-04 three ways. (a) A script that prints and exits 7: run directly -> `EXIT7-CHILD-RAN`, exit 7; run as `python tools/run_with_repo_venv.py <script>` -> **exit 0**, with the child's stdout arriving AFTER the calling shell had already moved on, and a redirect of that run capturing 0 bytes. (b) `python tools/run_with_repo_venv.py tools/check_institutional_correctness.py --enforced-only` -> exit 0, 0 bytes of output, while `python -m tools.check_institutional_correctness --enforced-only` on the SAME tree -> exit 1, `FAIL (2 enforced violation(s))`. (c) The proof it already cost us: commit 7646d3b4 minutes ago carried a 9-cell RC-252 row (an interior pipe in `static/css` + `js` + `icons`) and an RC-251 'dead end' with no NEXT-DEPTH; running the checks against `git show HEAD:governance/root_cause_log.md` reports rc_log_rows_keep_schema 1 violation at line 262 and no_terminal_null 1 at line 263 - both ENFORCED, both present in the committed blob, and the hook said Passed. | (1) why did an ENFORCED gate not block a commit that violates it? the hook's exit code came from the wrapper, not from the gate -> (2) why did the wrapper return 0? it uses os.execv, which on Windows spawns a detached child and returns control at once, so there was no exit status left to propagate -> (3) why was that never noticed? the failure is silent and green - a no-op gate and a passing gate are indistinguishable from the outside, and the hook only ever printed on failure, so 'no output, Passed' looked exactly like health -> (4) why did the local suites not cover it? every test drives the gate FUNCTIONS or invokes the module directly with `-m`; nothing executes the wrapper as pre-commit executes it, so the seam between hook and gate was the one path never exercised -> (5) ROOT: the wrapper's contract was written for POSIX exec semantics and adopted verbatim on Windows, where the call is a different primitive; nothing asserted the property the wrapper exists to provide - that a failing target makes the caller fail - so the gate's authority rested on an untested platform assumption. | PLAN: replace os.execv with a subprocess call whose returncode is propagated, in BOTH tools/run_with_repo_venv.py and tools/precommit_institutional.py, keeping stdout/stderr inherited so hook output still reaches the operator; add a control that runs the wrapper on a target exiting non-zero and asserts the code arrives, since that is the exact property no existing test asserted; then re-run the enforced gate honestly and repair whatever it has been silently passing. Re-run the whole diagnosis with `python tools/run_with_repo_venv.py <a script that exits 7>` (must report 7, reported 0 before the fix) and `python tools/precommit_institutional.py` (must carry the gate's own exit code), and cross-check against `python -m tools.check_institutional_correctness --enforced-only`. FIXED: tools/run_with_repo_venv.py replaces all three os.execv hand-offs with `_run`, a subprocess.run that inherits stdout/stderr and RETURNS the target's returncode; tools/precommit_institutional.py replaces its own os.execv with the same, so the gate's exit status becomes the hook's; tests/test_venv_wrapper_propagates_exit_v1.py is new and drives the wrapper the way pre-commit drives it. END-TO-END: pre-commit hook -> precommit_institutional -> run_with_repo_venv -> check_institutional_correctness --enforced-only -> returncode -> pre-commit BLOCKS. MEASURED and VERIFIED same-turn: `python tools/run_with_repo_venv.py <script exiting 7>` now reports **7** where it reported 0, and its child's output now arrives before the caller returns instead of after; `python tools/precommit_institutional.py` reported exit **1** with `INSTITUTIONAL CORRECTNESS GATE: FAIL (3 enforced violation(s))` - the first time on this host that hook has ever been able to say no. Those three were real and are repaired in the same change: RC-252's 9-cell row (an interior pipe in a path list), RC-251 declaring a dead end with no NEXT-DEPTH, and RC-249 asserting line numbers with no re-runnable citation - the last one authored while the gate was silently green, which is how it slipped in. `python -m tools.check_institutional_correctness --enforced-only` now returns **PASS (enforced checks clean)**, and the hook agrees with it by construction. Evidence: `python -m pytest tests/test_venv_wrapper_propagates_exit_v1.py -q` -> 5 passed, asserting that a non-zero exit reaches the caller, that the target's output is not lost to a detached race, that a clean target still reads clean, that no os.exec* CALL NODE remains on either hook file (AST, not text - the first draft matched the string and tripped on its own explanatory docstring, the RC-253 class again), and that the hook's exit and the gate's exit agree whatever the tree's state. RECURRENCE: this is the deepest instance yet of the shape named in RC-238, RC-241, RC-250 and RC-252 - a mechanism exists, and its REACH is assumed rather than measured. Here the mechanism was the repo's central lock and the unmeasured reach was whether its verdict could travel one process boundary. SCOPE: the same wrapper fronts venv-parity, market-correctness and operating-process, so all four pre-commit hooks regain their authority from this one fix. VIOLATION: NO-LOCK-EXISTED for the property that actually mattered. The repo owns `venv_parity`, `scheduled_producers_are_not_inert` and `enforced_checks_have_negative_controls`, and every one of them asks whether a check is CORRECT or REGISTERED - none asks whether its verdict can reach the process that acts on it. `enforced_checks_have_negative_controls` is the closest, and it is satisfied by a control that calls the check function, which is exactly the level at which this defect was invisible. TIGHTENED: tests/test_venv_wrapper_propagates_exit_v1.py binds the seam itself - a non-zero exit must reach the caller, the hook's exit must AGREE with the gate's exit whatever the verdict, and no os.exec* call node may return to either hook file (AST). The agreement test is the general one: it fails for any future change that decouples the hook's answer from the gate's, not just for this mechanism. NOT CLAIMED: I have not audited which past commits would have been blocked had the gate worked; the ledger is large and that is separate archaeology, not something to assert from here. NEXT-DEPTH: that archaeology is the unlock, and it is tractable rather than hopeless - replay `python -m tools.check_institutional_correctness --enforced-only` against `git show <sha>:governance/root_cause_log.md` for the commits made on this host since the wrapper was introduced, which turns 'the gate was silent' from a worry into a list. The phrase 'dead end' above is a QUOTATION of the RC-251 violation this row's fix surfaced, not a claim that any road here ends. |

(paths: math_levels.py, reports/, server.py, static/)` for a `git commit` invocation, a subcommand that cannot destroy a working tree under any flags. This is the exact failure RC-93 named - ban the ACTION, not the word - and it lands on the honesty path specifically: the more precisely a commit message or RC row describes a destructive-git incident, the more certainly the guard refuses to record it, which pressures every future writer toward vaguer accounts of exactly the incidents this repo most needs written down. | (1) why did a `git commit` BLOCK? _RESET_GUARD_RE searches the entire command string for a destructive verb, so an occurrence inside a heredoc body matched -> (2) why does the whole string get searched? the guard was written against short one-line commands where command and payload are the same text, and no case distinguished the git SUBCOMMAND from data the command merely carries -> (3) why is that wrong in principle? a heredoc body and a quoted -m message are DATA fed to a non-destructive subcommand; judging them is a category error, and the guard's own message says paths are 'touched' when nothing is touched -> (4) why is this worse than an ordinary false positive? it fires hardest on accurate incident write-ups, so the safe way to get a commit through is to describe the wipe vaguely - a lock that taxes precision in the ledger corrodes the record it exists to protect -> (5) ROOT: the guard's input was the raw command text rather than the command's ACTION, so payload and instruction were indistinguishable to it; RC-93 was encoded as a naming rule for guards but never as an input-normalisation requirement, leaving every string-matching guard free to re-introduce word-banning. | PLAN: normalise the guard's input before matching - strip heredoc bodies and quoted -m/--message payloads, judge the remainder - while refusing to strip when the command hands the heredoc to an interpreter (bash/sh/pwsh/powershell/eval/xargs), since there the body IS the instruction. Controls must assert both directions: a commit message quoting destructive git passes, and the same verbs as a real command, or piped into a shell, still block. FIXED: tools/operating_process_lock.py gains `_strip_command_payloads`, which removes heredoc bodies (`_HEREDOC_RE`) and quoted -m/--message/--file/-F payloads (`_MESSAGE_PAYLOAD_RE`) before any matching, and `reset_guard_violations` now matches BOTH the destructive-verb regex and the protected-path scan against that normalised text, so payload can no longer be mistaken for instruction. The exemption is deliberately narrow: `_INTERPRETER_RE` detects a command that hands its body to bash/sh/zsh/pwsh/powershell/cmd/eval/xargs/source, and in that case NOTHING is stripped, because there the heredoc body is the instruction. END-TO-END: PreToolUse Bash -> tools/process_lock_guard.py -> reset_guard_violations -> _strip_command_payloads -> verb and path match on the ACTION only -> BLOCK or allow. MEASURED and VERIFIED same-turn: the `git commit --file -` heredoc that this very row's commit needed - a message body containing three destructive-git strings - now passes, while `bash <<EOF / git reset --hard / EOF` still BLOCKS, `git commit -m '<msg>' && git checkout -- server.py` still BLOCKS on the second command (proving the strip does not swallow what follows it), and every real destructive form from the RC-231 and RC-252 batteries is unchanged. Evidence: `python -m pytest tests/test_reset_guard_v1.py tests/test_operating_process_lock_v1.py -q` -> 39 passed, including three RC-253 controls asserting both directions. SELF-DEMONSTRATING: this row exists because the guard refused the commit that describes RC-252, and the row's own text - which quotes the destructive forms verbatim - is only committable because of the fix it documents. That is the argument for the fix, not a coincidence: a lock that made accurate incident reporting the hard path was already corroding the ledger. RECURRENCE: RC-93 (ban the ACTION not the word) was previously encoded as a naming convention for guard messages; it is now an INPUT-NORMALISATION requirement in the one guard that reads raw command text, which is where the principle actually had to live. |

SERVED-PAGE probe of http://127.0.0.1:8000 (not a read of static/index.html): `GET /` -> 200, 637,114 bytes, `<title>` = **Ed Console** (unchanged, per the operator's instruction that there be no rename), `id="axiom-splash"` present, css/js/webmanifest all linked, and **exactly ONE** `AxiomSplash.dismiss()` occurrence in the SERVED markup - which is this row's entire point, since the defect it was opened for was a second dismissal reachable from the duplicate-fingerprint skip that paints nothing. Assets served: /static/css/axiom-splash.css 200 (6,811 B), /static/js/axiom-splash.js 200 (1,158 B), /static/site.webmanifest 200 (800 B), /static/icons/axiom.ico 200 (21,698 B), /static/icons/axiom-512.png 200 (19,507 B). RENDERED-FRAME probe in a real browser against the live app, which is what the ship-confirmation law actually demands: `window.AxiomSplash` exposes exactly `["dismiss"]`; after a reload the overlay was IN THE DOM at full opacity through 15.153s, took the `ax-out` fade class at 15.769s, and was REMOVED at 16.392s - a 623ms fade matching the 600ms CSS transition. So the splash covers the screen until an accepted full render and then leaves, which is the contract; it does not lift on DOMContentLoaded (552ms) or on load (577ms), both of which passed roughly fifteen seconds before dismissal. END-TO-END: browser -> GET / -> splash overlay as first body child -> _renderMoneyPathCore accepted branch -> AxiomSplash.dismiss() -> ax-out fade -> element removed. Evidence: `python -m pytest tests/test_axiom_brand_landing_v1.py -q` -> 9 passed. FINDING THAT OUTLIVES THIS ROW, recorded because it contradicts a number I repeated: the startup budget in circulation treats the splash as a ~5.8s floor (5.2s animation + 0.6s fade) and therefore as the dominant term of perceived startup on a warm server. The live frame says the overlay actually stood for **at least 16.4s** on a warm, already-serving console - the 5.2s is only the ANIMATION floor, while dismissal is gated on the first accepted full render, which took about fifteen seconds. The splash is not hiding a ready console; it is honestly reporting one that is not ready. Shortening it would uncover an unpainted screen, which is the exact hazard this row was opened to prevent. POWER, declared rather than assumed: the CLOSURE evidence is deterministic and needs no interval - the served markup either contains one dismiss site or it does not (it contains exactly one), each asset either returns 200 with the expected byte count or it does not (all five do), and the overlay either leaves the DOM after the accepted render or it does not (it left, at 16.392s, after taking the fade class at 15.769s). The TIMING observation is different in kind and its verdict is softened to UNPROVEN, deliberately: n=1 page load, no repetition and therefore no 95% CI and no power figure, on a warm console with a browser and other work competing, so it establishes that the overlay CAN stand far past the 5.2s animation floor and refutes the claim that 5.8s is the whole term, but it does not estimate the typical time to first accepted render. That estimate is owed by the quiet-host protocol named below and must not be quoted from this single frame. NEXT-DEPTH: that ~15s to first accepted render is the real target and is the operator's next mission (SERVER COLD START), to be measured on a QUIET host with the console down, >=7 runs, import and listen and health timed separately - not by touching splash timing, which the operator has explicitly frozen. VIOLATION: NO-LOCK-EXISTED at the time the spec was written. The integration instruction said dismiss on "_renderMoneyPathCore success return true", and nothing in the repo asked whether a named RETURN VALUE has more than one producing branch - the UI-mockup lock governs registered surfaces, not the semantics of a callback contract, and static/index.html is not a registered surface. The duplicate-fingerprint skip returns the same token having painted nothing, so a literal implementation would have uncovered an unpainted console on any repeat Tier-C payload. TIGHTENED: tests/test_axiom_brand_landing_v1.py pins the dismissal to the accepted-render branch and asserts its ABSENCE from the four short-circuit paths (DOMContentLoaded, EventSource onopen, the quote lane, the dedup-skip return), so re-introducing a second dismissal fails a test rather than shipping; the served-markup probe used for this closure counts dismiss sites in the RENDERED page, which is the form a future reviewer can re-run in one command without reading the file. |

| RC-247 | CLOSED | 2026-08-04 | 2026-08-05 | THE CONSOLE PAYS SKLEARN'S IMPORT COST AT EVERY BOOT TO SERVE A PATH THAT NEVER CALLS IT: `arch_competition/metrics.py` imports `from sklearn.metrics import balanced_accuracy_score, log_loss` at module level; `arch_competition/__init__.py` eagerly imports eval_runner, which imports metrics; and the live console reaches that package from `calibration.v2_a1_conformal` -> `calibration.v2_a1_calibration` -> `arch_competition`. NOTE FOR HONESTY: my earlier claim that torch (6.10 s) and xgboost (2.60 s) load at boot was WRONG and measurement-order contaminated; a fresh process shows both are ALREADY lazy, and the real boot tax is sklearn+scipy. FIXED: arch_competition/metrics.py `_sklearn_metrics()` import-on-use accessor replacing the module-level `from sklearn.metrics import ...`, with all FOUR call sites converted - half_split_log_loss_std, the balanced_accuracy slice summary, the per-window log_loss, and architecture_win_consistency_by_window where the accessor is hoisted OUTSIDE the slice loop. END-TO-END: import server -> calibration.v2_a1_conformal -> arch_competition -> eval_runner -> metrics (pure numpy only) -> sklearn NOT loaded; a scoring caller enters one of the four functions -> accessor imports sklearn once -> same numbers as before. MEASURED and VERIFIED this turn in FRESH processes (the only honest way to time an import): `import server` 17.89s BEFORE -> 6.61s AFTER, 11.28s saved (63%), and the heavy-library set at boot goes from numpy+pandas+scipy+sklearn to numpy alone. VIOLATION: the console spent 10.3 s of every boot importing a scoring library it never calls on the serve path, and no control asked whether a package __init__ was charging its consumers for its heaviest subject - the cost was invisible because import time is nobody's assertion. TIGHTENED: the import boundary is now itself attacked, in a subprocess, so a future module-level sklearn import fails a test rather than quietly costing 10 s per start. Evidence: `python -m pytest tests/test_arch_competition_metrics.py -q` -> 20 passed (and 95 across the five arch-competition and stack-bundle suites run together this turn), including two new RC-247 controls - one asserting in a SUBPROCESS (so the ambient session's imports cannot mask a regression) that importing arch_competition.metrics leaves sklearn and scipy unloaded, and one proving the lazy accessor still delivers log_loss so laziness did not become absence. MEASUREMENT CORRECTION 2026-08-05, filed here because this is where the claim lives rather than as a new open item (the ledger may only shrink): the FIX holds, but the NUMBER was reported with a precision the sample cannot support. The PM's startup audit read 8.6-12.5s (median 10.5s) and called 6.61s non-reproducing. Two things are true. First, they are different clocks - 6.61s is `import server` in a fresh process, the PM's span is launch-before-listen and additionally carries interpreter start, uvicorn and app construction. Second, and larger: on this host with the console UP, ten fresh-process runs of the IDENTICAL command gave 12.99, 6.01, 9.15, 11.77, 8.24, 12.00, 18.50, 18.64, 14.57, 13.07 - min 6.01, max 18.64, a spread over 12 SECONDS, rising as the sequence proceeded, with the bare-interpreter control itself drifting 2.538 -> 0.731 -> 0.312s. A spread that wide cannot adjudicate an 11.28s improvement, and 6.61 sits INSIDE the range rather than outside it, so neither 'does not reproduce' nor a bare '6.61' is supportable; the act of measuring while the console served perturbed what was measured. Re-run with `python -c "import subprocess,sys,time; t=time.perf_counter(); subprocess.run([sys.executable,'-c','import server'],capture_output=True); print(round(time.perf_counter()-t,2))"` repeatedly, and note the machine state. What IS stable is COMPOSITION, and it confirms the repair: `python -X importtime -c "import server"` on a 13.43s run attributes fastapi 3.91s (fastapi.params 2.05, openapi.models 0.83), schwab_client -> schwab.auth -> authlib 2.00s, asyncio 1.46s, server's own body 1.70s, calibration.writer 1.01s, shutil 0.74s, time_et 0.73s - with NO sklearn and NO scipy anywhere in the tree, which is precisely what this row fixed. It also says the next lever is diffuse: no single villain remains, the largest item being fastapi/pydantic at roughly a third, which is structural rather than a lazy-import win. VIOLATION (second-order, this row's own reporting): the repo has a hard law that a number cite a re-runnable COMMAND and no law that it cite its SAMPLE - n, spread, and what else was running - so a one-run timing and a converged one are typed identically in the ledger. TIGHTENED: not by a new check today; the honest interim is that every timing figure in this row now carries its n and its spread, and the controlled quiet-host protocol (console DOWN, >=7 runs per stage, import/listen/health timed separately, bare-interpreter control each round) is specified in the PM's startup audit where the remaining cold-path question belongs. |

Ship controls proving the pre-commit path excludes advisory, that advisory mode still RUNS all seven, and that no advisory check can silently leave the catalogue. FIXED: tools/check_institutional_correctness.py `run_checks(mode=...)` plus `--enforced-only` / `--advisory` in main() and `write_advisory_report`, and tools/precommit_institutional.py now execs the gate with --enforced-only. Default (no flag) is UNCHANGED, so a human running the gate by hand still sees all 67. The advisory run writes reports/advisory_debt_latest.json with a UTC timestamp and a per-check count, which is the visibility half the PM's approval was conditional on - the debt moved off the blocking path, it did not disappear. END-TO-END: git commit -> pre-commit -> precommit_institutional --enforced-only -> 60 ENFORCED checks only -> BLOCK or pass; separately, scheduled/rehab -> --advisory -> 7 checks -> dated JSON a human and the rehab scan can read. MEASURED and VERIFIED this turn: blocking path 100.0s versus 244.9s for the full catalogue, a 145s saving on every commit (59%), with enforced coverage unchanged at 60 checks. Evidence: `python -m pytest tests/test_enforced_check_negative_controls_v1.py -q` -> 30 passed, including four new RC-246 controls - the wrapper really asks for --enforced-only, enforced mode SKIPS advisory checks, advisory mode still RUNS them and writes the dated record, and all seven remain REGISTERED because moving them off the hook is not permission to delete them. |

Mandatory: numerical equivalence versus the current implementation on real banked chains - charm is a money-path surface serving /api/forces - plus before/after milliseconds the PM can re-verify. FIXED: math_exposure_core.compute_net_charm - `now` resolved ONCE per call (so every contract in an aggregate is priced at one instant) and T memoised per distinct expirationDate via `_tte_memo`; the bs_charm faucet is untouched, so RC-224's one-formula rule still holds. END-TO-END: banked chain -> compute_net_charm -> single pinned instant -> one T per expiry -> bs_charm per contract -> dealer-signed aggregate -> /api/forces charm_below/charm_above. MEASURED and VERIFIED this turn: numerical equivalence against the SHIPPED HEAD implementation (loaded from `git show HEAD:math_exposure_core.py`, not from memory) over 12 real banked chains with a pinned instant -> identical=12 mismatched=0, every key bit-for-bit; timing, both warm, n=30 -> BEFORE 1.39 ms/call, AFTER 0.57 ms/call, 2.42x, 59% saved on a 188-contract chain. THE PERFORMANCE PREMISE OF P5 WAS WRONG AND I AM THE ONE WHO GOT IT WRONG - stated plainly because the PM will re-verify: I reported 70.9 ms to the operator and to Cursor, which was a COLD figure amortising a 2.62 s one-time lazy import of time_et/zoneinfo into a 3-run average; my corrected profile then said 23.3 ms, which was cProfile's own instrumentation overhead; the TRUE warm cost is 1.39 ms per call, so charm was never a hot spot at any point and no numpy vectorisation was ever warranted. What justifies keeping this change is the CORRECTNESS half, which measurement surfaced by accident: with now=None the old code re-read the clock inside the loop, so contracts summed into ONE published number were priced at instants drifting across the loop - a single figure that did not describe a single moment. RECURRENCE: measurement-before-action was followed but not CAREFULLY enough - three successive measurements each overturned the last, and the first two both reached the operator as fact; structurally different this time in that the row now records the contaminated figures alongside the clean one, so the correction is auditable rather than quietly replaced. |
